I do some library tutoring preparation, or at least I’m supposed to, and I’ll find myself not starting it because I feel I have to settle the condo things first. Same with hobbies — things I used to just sit down and do, I’ll delay because there’s this sense that I can’t move on until I’m sure enough about whatever I was checking.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting my days. It’s not ruining everything, but it eats up time and makes ordinary tasks feel much heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you need to perform certain actions a specific number of times, in a certain way, or until it feels 'just right,' even if it's not directly related to what you're worried about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the “until it feels right” part fits more than a particular number. I don’t usually say to myself, “I have to do this three times,” or anything as clear as that. It’s more that I check the same boxes, or the same list, or the condo drawers again and again until I can somehow let myself move on.\n\nAnd that’s what really brought me in, honestly. I’ll know perfectly well that I already looked. I can remember opening the drawer, seeing what was inside, closing it. But then I’ll be halfway to the door, or my wife will be standing there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I get this pull to go back and make sure one more time. Not because there’s some new reason, exactly. Just because it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know it looks unnecessary, and I know it is unnecessary in a practical sense. But in the moment, leaving it unchecked feels harder than going back and doing it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to arrange things in a certain way, make sure things are perfectly aligned, or repeat actions until they feel exactly right, even if it’s not about checking for safety or preventing something bad?\n\nPatient: Not so much the arranging or lining things up, no. I don’t really have a thing where objects have to be perfectly straight or symmetrical, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nFor me it’s much more the repeating until I can move on. What brought me in is that I keep finding myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same condo drawers over and over. I know perfectly well I already looked — I can remember looking — but it still doesn’t feel settled. My wife might be standing there waiting for me, and I know she wants me to just leave it be, and I want that too. But I’ll still feel pulled back to check it again, almost like I need that last look before I can go on to the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don’t want or welcome—thoughts that feel intrusive or upsetting, even if you’re not always acting on them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. They’re not dramatic thoughts, exactly, but they’re intrusive in the sense that I don’t invite them and I can’t seem to just dismiss them when they show up.\n\nA common one is this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files — some paper or note or folder that I’ll need later, even if I haven’t needed it in years. Or I’ll think maybe I put a tool in the wrong box while unpacking, and then the thought just sits there and bothers me. I’ll tell myself, “Well, that’s probably not true,” or “If it were really important, I’d know,” but it doesn’t quite take. It comes back around again, and then I have trouble settling down and moving on to whatever I was trying to do next.\n\nIt’s upsetting mostly because it feels unreasonable, and I know that. I can argue with it logically, but it still keeps tapping me on the shoulder, so to speak.\n\nDoctor: When you have these intrusive thoughts, do you feel driven to do something — like checking, repeating, or reassuring yourself — to try to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly the pattern, I think. The thought comes in, and then I feel like I have to do something to quiet it down — usually checking.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, and the drawers at the condo over and over before I can move on. And the odd thing is, I do know I already looked. I’m not confused about it. I can remember looking in the drawer, or going through the list, or checking the box, and still it feels like there’s this unfinished feeling, like I can’t quite trust it unless I look again.\n\nIt’s not that my wife hasn’t been patient, but she’ll be standing there waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I can see how unnecessary it looks from the outside. I know perfectly well that I already checked. But in the moment, checking again feels like the only way to get that anxious feeling to settle enough so I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to throw things away or feel a need to keep items longer than you think you reasonably should, even if they don’t seem useful to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as someone who keeps things just for the sake of keeping them. I mean, I have old papers and household things like anyone my age would, and sometimes I’ll hesitate before throwing something out, especially if it seems like it might have some future use. But I wouldn’t say I have piles of useless things that I can’t part with, or that rooms are taken over by belongings, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWith teaching materials, for example, there are things I’ve kept longer than someone else might think is necessary, but in my mind they had some possible value or history attached to them. So I don’t know if that counts. It doesn’t feel like collecting or hoarding to me. It’s more that I can get stuck wondering whether something might turn out to matter later, and then I’m slower to toss it. But as a general need to keep everything, no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to get rid of items, even ones that seem useless or have no real value, because the idea of throwing them away feels unsettling or causes you distress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that way. I mean, I can hesitate with things, especially if I get into that frame of mind of, “Well, what if I need this later?” But if something is truly useless to me, or has no real value that I can see, I don’t usually feel distressed in the sense of being unable to let it go.\n\nSometimes I may take longer than I should deciding, because I’ll sort of review what it is, where it came from, whether it belongs with other papers or household items, that sort of thing. My wife would probably say I can make too much of a small decision. But it’s not that the object itself feels impossible to part with. It’s more the uncertainty around whether I’m making the right decision.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I have a strong distress about throwing away useless things just because they’re being thrown away. It’s more that I can get bogged down in deciding whether they really are useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you have to perform certain mental acts—like counting, praying, or going over something in your mind—a certain way or a certain number of times to prevent something bad from happening or to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not so much counting or praying, no. It’s more… going back over it in my head only seems to happen around the checking. Like with the boxes or the lists or the drawers at the condo, I’ll know I already looked, and I can even picture myself having looked. But then my mind sort of replays it and says, “Are you sure? Did you really see it properly?” And then I feel pushed to check again.\n\nIt isn’t usually a set number, like three times or seven times. It’s more that I don’t feel done until it feels settled, if that makes sense. My wife will be waiting for me, and I’ll know she wants me to just leave it be, and part of me agrees with her completely. But the anxious feeling doesn’t really let go until I’ve checked again, even though I know perfectly well I already did.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to discard items, even ones that seem to have no real value, because you feel a strong need to save them or because it causes you significant distress to throw them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s really a big issue for me, no. I mean, I may pause over things longer than is sensible, especially papers or old household items where I’m thinking, “Well, maybe this could be useful,” or “Maybe I should check what this is before tossing it.” But it’s not usually because I feel some powerful need to save it, or because throwing it away feels intolerable.\n\nIf something really has no value and I can identify it as trash, I can get rid of it. I might take a little too long deciding, and sometimes I’ll second-guess whether I’ve sorted it correctly, but I don’t feel deeply attached to the item itself. It’s more that I like to be sure I’m not making a mistake.\n\nSo, in the way you’re asking it—significant distress about discarding, even useless things—I’d say no, I haven’t noticed that in myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to get rid of items, even ones that seem useless or have no real value, because the idea of throwing them away feels unsettling or causes you distress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that way. I mean, I can hesitate with things, especially if I get into that frame of mind of, “Well, what if I need this later?” But if something is truly useless to me, or has no real value that I can see, I don’t usually feel distressed in the sense of being unable to let it go.\n\nSometimes I may take longer than I should deciding, because I’ll sort of review what it is, where it came from, whether it belongs with other papers or household items, that sort of thing. My wife would probably say I can make too much of a small decision. But it’s not that the object itself feels impossible to part with. It’s more the uncertainty around whether I’m making the right decision.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I have a strong distress about throwing away useless things just because they’re being thrown away. It’s more that I can get bogged down in deciding whether they really are useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you have to perform certain mental acts—like counting, praying, or going over something in your mind—a certain way or a certain number of times to prevent something bad from happening or to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not so much counting or praying, no. It’s more… going back over it in my head only seems to happen around the checking. Like with the boxes or the lists or the drawers at the condo, I’ll know I already looked, and I can even picture myself having looked. But then my mind sort of replays it and says, “Are you sure? Did you really see it properly?” And then I feel pushed to check again.\n\nIt isn’t usually a set number, like three times or seven times. It’s more that I don’t feel done until it feels settled, if that makes sense. My wife will be waiting for me, and I’ll know she wants me to just leave it be, and part of me agrees with her completely. But the anxious feeling doesn’t really let go until I’ve checked again, even though I know perfectly well I already did.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to discard items, even ones that seem to have no real value, because you feel a strong need to save them or because it causes you significant distress to throw them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s really a big issue for me, no. I mean, I may pause over things longer than is sensible, especially papers or old household items where I’m thinking, “Well, maybe this could be useful,” or “Maybe I should check what this is before tossing it.” But it’s not usually because I feel some powerful need to save it, or because throwing it away feels intolerable.\n\nIf something really has no value and I can identify it as trash, I can get rid of it. I might take a little too long deciding, and sometimes I’ll second-guess whether I’ve sorted it correctly, but I don’t feel deeply attached to the item itself. It’s more that I like to be sure I’m not making a mistake.\n\nSo, in the way you’re asking it—significant distress about discarding, even useless things—I’d say no, I haven’t noticed that in myself.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever find yourself repeating words, phrases, or numbers in your head, or feel the need to do something mentally a certain way to reduce anxiety, even if it's not connected to checking or organizing physical objects?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 76 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Compulsions\nmanifestation: \"What brought me in is that I find myself checking the same boxes, lists, and condo drawers over and over before I can move on, even though I know perfectly well I already looked and my wife is waiting for me to just leave it be.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in a separate way, no. I mean, I can’t think of myself sitting there repeating a word or a number in my head to make something feel safe. The thing that keeps happening—the thing that really brought me in—is still tied to checking.

It’s those boxes, or the lists, or the drawers at the condo. I’ll look once, and I’ll know I looked. Then I’ll be ready to move on, or at least I’ll think I’m ready, and then this doubt comes back in: “Did I actually check that properly?” And before I can leave it alone, I end up checking again. Sometimes my wife is right there waiting for me, and I know she wants me to just let it be, and honestly I want to let it be too. But I don’t quite feel able to move on until I’ve gone back over it.

So I wouldn’t say it’s words or numbers in my head, exactly. It’s more that mental uncertainty that pushes me back into checking the same things, even when I know perfectly well I already did.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F42.1, F42.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, what finally made me come in is this business of checking things, and I know that sounds small when I say it out loud, but it’s become... it’s become hard to just go about my day. I’ll check the same boxes, or lists I’ve made, or drawers in the condo, and then I’ll have to check them again before I can move on. And the frustrating part is, I know perfectly well I already looked. I can remember looking. But somehow that doesn’t settle it.\n\nMy wife will be standing there waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I want to, I really do, but I get caught up in making sure one more time. Then one more after that. It’s not that anything dramatic happens, exactly, but it slows everything down and makes me feel rather foolish and stuck. That’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Besides checking things, do you find yourself having other repetitive thoughts or feeling like you have to perform certain actions a specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I’m not sure if it’s exactly the same sort of thing. It’s more these thoughts that get stuck in my head, nagging at me. For example, I’ll start thinking I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files — some paper or note or record that I might need, even though realistically I know I probably don’t need it anymore. Or I’ll worry that I put a tool in the wrong box, and then later I won’t be able to find it when I need it.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can tell myself, “Now, come on, that’s unlikely,” or “You would remember if you’d thrown that out,” but it doesn’t quite land. The thought comes back again, and then I feel like I can’t really settle down or move on until I’ve somehow reassured myself about it. It’s not always loud, if that makes sense, but it’s persistent. It sort of keeps tapping me on the shoulder.\n\nDoctor: When you have these recurring thoughts, do they lead you to do certain things—like checking, organizing, or repeating actions—to try to feel better or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what happens, I suppose. The thought will come up, and then I feel I have to do something with it, not just let it sit there. So I’ll go back and check the same box, or the same list, or a drawer in the condo, even if I’ve already opened it and looked inside.\n\nAnd I know I’ve already looked — that’s the maddening part. It isn’t that I’ve forgotten in the usual sense. I can remember standing there and checking it. But there’s still this feeling of, “Well, maybe just make sure once more,” and then once more after that. It’s as if I can’t quite move on to the next thing until I’ve done it enough times, though I couldn’t tell you what “enough” is.\n\nMy wife will be waiting for me, sometimes literally standing there ready to go, and I can see that she wants me to just leave it alone. I want to leave it alone too. But I get pulled back into checking, and it’s embarrassing, really, because part of me is watching myself do it and thinking, “You already did this.” But I still feel I have to check before I can settle.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and checking behaviors cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life, such as taking up a lot of time or affecting your work or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does interfere, though I’d call it more wearing and time-consuming than catastrophic, if that distinction makes sense. The biggest example lately is unpacking the condo. That should have been a fairly ordinary job — boxes, shelves, sorting things into drawers — but it has stretched on much longer than it had any right to, because I keep stopping to verify what’s in a box, or where I put something, or whether I put it in the correct place.\n\nAnd then because I haven’t “finished” that, or because it doesn’t feel finished enough, I put off other things. I do some library tutoring preparation, or at least I’m supposed to, and I’ll find myself not starting it because I feel I have to settle the condo things first. Same with hobbies — things I used to just sit down and do, I’ll delay because there’s this sense that I can’t move on until I’m sure enough about whatever I was checking.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting my days. It’s not ruining everything, but it eats up time and makes ordinary tasks feel much heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you need to perform certain actions a specific number of times, in a certain way, or until it feels 'just right,' even if it's not directly related to what you're worried about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the “until it feels right” part fits more than a particular number. I don’t usually say to myself, “I have to do this three times,” or anything as clear as that. It’s more that I check the same boxes, or the same list, or the condo drawers again and again until I can somehow let myself move on.\n\nAnd that’s what really brought me in, honestly. I’ll know perfectly well that I already looked. I can remember opening the drawer, seeing what was inside, closing it. But then I’ll be halfway to the door, or my wife will be standing there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I get this pull to go back and make sure one more time. Not because there’s some new reason, exactly. Just because it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know it looks unnecessary, and I know it is unnecessary in a practical sense. But in the moment, leaving it unchecked feels harder than going back and doing it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to arrange things in a certain way, make sure things are perfectly aligned, or repeat actions until they feel exactly right, even if it’s not about checking for safety or preventing something bad?\n\nPatient: Not so much the arranging or lining things up, no. I don’t really have a thing where objects have to be perfectly straight or symmetrical, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nFor me it’s much more the repeating until I can move on. What brought me in is that I keep finding myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same condo drawers over and over. I know perfectly well I already looked — I can remember looking — but it still doesn’t feel settled. My wife might be standing there waiting for me, and I know she wants me to just leave it be, and I want that too. But I’ll still feel pulled back to check it again, almost like I need that last look before I can go on to the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don’t want or welcome—thoughts that feel intrusive or upsetting, even if you’re not always acting on them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. They’re not dramatic thoughts, exactly, but they’re intrusive in the sense that I don’t invite them and I can’t seem to just dismiss them when they show up.\n\nA common one is this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files — some paper or note or folder that I’ll need later, even if I haven’t needed it in years. Or I’ll think maybe I put a tool in the wrong box while unpacking, and then the thought just sits there and bothers me. I’ll tell myself, “Well, that’s probably not true,” or “If it were really important, I’d know,” but it doesn’t quite take. It comes back around again, and then I have trouble settling down and moving on to whatever I was trying to do next.\n\nIt’s upsetting mostly because it feels unreasonable, and I know that. I can argue with it logically, but it still keeps tapping me on the shoulder, so to speak.\n\nDoctor: When you have these intrusive thoughts, do you feel driven to do something — like checking, repeating, or reassuring yourself — to try to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly the pattern, I think. The thought comes in, and then I feel like I have to do something to quiet it down — usually checking.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, and the drawers at the condo over and over before I can move on. And the odd thing is, I do know I already looked. I’m not confused about it. I can remember looking in the drawer, or going through the list, or checking the box, and still it feels like there’s this unfinished feeling, like I can’t quite trust it unless I look again.\n\nIt’s not that my wife hasn’t been patient, but she’ll be standing there waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I can see how unnecessary it looks from the outside. I know perfectly well that I already checked. But in the moment, checking again feels like the only way to get that anxious feeling to settle enough so I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to throw things away or feel a need to keep items longer than you think you reasonably should, even if they don’t seem useful to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as someone who keeps things just for the sake of keeping them. I mean, I have old papers and household things like anyone my age would, and sometimes I’ll hesitate before throwing something out, especially if it seems like it might have some future use. But I wouldn’t say I have piles of useless things that I can’t part with, or that rooms are taken over by belongings, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWith teaching materials, for example, there are things I’ve kept longer than someone else might think is necessary, but in my mind they had some possible value or history attached to them. So I don’t know if that counts. It doesn’t feel like collecting or hoarding to me. It’s more that I can get stuck wondering whether something might turn out to matter later, and then I’m slower to toss it. But as a general need to keep everything, no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to get rid of items, even ones that seem useless or have no real value, because the idea of throwing them away feels unsettling or causes you distress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that way. I mean, I can hesitate with things, especially if I get into that frame of mind of, “Well, what if I need this later?” But if something is truly useless to me, or has no real value that I can see, I don’t usually feel distressed in the sense of being unable to let it go.\n\nSometimes I may take longer than I should deciding, because I’ll sort of review what it is, where it came from, whether it belongs with other papers or household items, that sort of thing. My wife would probably say I can make too much of a small decision. But it’s not that the object itself feels impossible to part with. It’s more the uncertainty around whether I’m making the right decision.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I have a strong distress about throwing away useless things just because they’re being thrown away. It’s more that I can get bogged down in deciding whether they really are useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you have to perform certain mental acts—like counting, praying, or going over something in your mind—a certain way or a certain number of times to prevent something bad from happening or to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not so much counting or praying, no. It’s more… going back over it in my head only seems to happen around the checking. Like with the boxes or the lists or the drawers at the condo, I’ll know I already looked, and I can even picture myself having looked. But then my mind sort of replays it and says, “Are you sure? Did you really see it properly?” And then I feel pushed to check again.\n\nIt isn’t usually a set number, like three times or seven times. It’s more that I don’t feel done until it feels settled, if that makes sense. My wife will be waiting for me, and I’ll know she wants me to just leave it be, and part of me agrees with her completely. But the anxious feeling doesn’t really let go until I’ve checked again, even though I know perfectly well I already did.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to discard items, even ones that seem to have no real value, because you feel a strong need to save them or because it causes you significant distress to throw them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s really a big issue for me, no. I mean, I may pause over things longer than is sensible, especially papers or old household items where I’m thinking, “Well, maybe this could be useful,” or “Maybe I should check what this is before tossing it.” But it’s not usually because I feel some powerful need to save it, or because throwing it away feels intolerable.\n\nIf something really has no value and I can identify it as trash, I can get rid of it. I might take a little too long deciding, and sometimes I’ll second-guess whether I’ve sorted it correctly, but I don’t feel deeply attached to the item itself. It’s more that I like to be sure I’m not making a mistake.\n\nSo, in the way you’re asking it—significant distress about discarding, even useless things—I’d say no, I haven’t noticed that in myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating words, phrases, or numbers in your head, or feel the need to do something mentally a certain way to reduce anxiety, even if it's not connected to checking or organizing physical objects?\n\nPatient: Not really in a separate way, no. I mean, I can’t think of myself sitting there repeating a word or a number in my head to make something feel safe. The thing that keeps happening—the thing that really brought me in—is still tied to checking.\n\nIt’s those boxes, or the lists, or the drawers at the condo. I’ll look once, and I’ll know I looked. Then I’ll be ready to move on, or at least I’ll think I’m ready, and then this doubt comes back in: “Did I actually check that properly?” And before I can leave it alone, I end up checking again. Sometimes my wife is right there waiting for me, and I know she wants me to just let it be, and honestly I want to let it be too. But I don’t quite feel able to move on until I’ve gone back over it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s words or numbers in my head, exactly. It’s more that mental uncertainty that pushes me back into checking the same things, even when I know perfectly well I already did.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And the odd thing is, I do know I already looked. I’m not confused about it. I can remember looking in the drawer, or going through the list, or checking the box, and still it feels like there’s this unfinished feeling, like I can’t quite trust it unless I look again.\n\nIt’s not that my wife hasn’t been patient, but she’ll be standing there waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I can see how unnecessary it looks from the outside. I know perfectly well that I already checked. But in the moment, checking again feels like the only way to get that anxious feeling to settle enough so I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to throw things away or feel a need to keep items longer than you think you reasonably should, even if they don’t seem useful to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as someone who keeps things just for the sake of keeping them. I mean, I have old papers and household things like anyone my age would, and sometimes I’ll hesitate before throwing something out, especially if it seems like it might have some future use. But I wouldn’t say I have piles of useless things that I can’t part with, or that rooms are taken over by belongings, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWith teaching materials, for example, there are things I’ve kept longer than someone else might think is necessary, but in my mind they had some possible value or history attached to them. So I don’t know if that counts. It doesn’t feel like collecting or hoarding to me. It’s more that I can get stuck wondering whether something might turn out to matter later, and then I’m slower to toss it. But as a general need to keep everything, no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to get rid of items, even ones that seem useless or have no real value, because the idea of throwing them away feels unsettling or causes you distress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that way. I mean, I can hesitate with things, especially if I get into that frame of mind of, “Well, what if I need this later?” But if something is truly useless to me, or has no real value that I can see, I don’t usually feel distressed in the sense of being unable to let it go.\n\nSometimes I may take longer than I should deciding, because I’ll sort of review what it is, where it came from, whether it belongs with other papers or household items, that sort of thing. My wife would probably say I can make too much of a small decision. But it’s not that the object itself feels impossible to part with. It’s more the uncertainty around whether I’m making the right decision.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I have a strong distress about throwing away useless things just because they’re being thrown away. It’s more that I can get bogged down in deciding whether they really are useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you have to perform certain mental acts—like counting, praying, or going over something in your mind—a certain way or a certain number of times to prevent something bad from happening or to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not so much counting or praying, no. It’s more… going back over it in my head only seems to happen around the checking. Like with the boxes or the lists or the drawers at the condo, I’ll know I already looked, and I can even picture myself having looked. But then my mind sort of replays it and says, “Are you sure? Did you really see it properly?” And then I feel pushed to check again.\n\nIt isn’t usually a set number, like three times or seven times. It’s more that I don’t feel done until it feels settled, if that makes sense. My wife will be waiting for me, and I’ll know she wants me to just leave it be, and part of me agrees with her completely. But the anxious feeling doesn’t really let go until I’ve checked again, even though I know perfectly well I already did.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to discard items, even ones that seem to have no real value, because you feel a strong need to save them or because it causes you significant distress to throw them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s really a big issue for me, no. I mean, I may pause over things longer than is sensible, especially papers or old household items where I’m thinking, “Well, maybe this could be useful,” or “Maybe I should check what this is before tossing it.” But it’s not usually because I feel some powerful need to save it, or because throwing it away feels intolerable.\n\nIf something really has no value and I can identify it as trash, I can get rid of it. I might take a little too long deciding, and sometimes I’ll second-guess whether I’ve sorted it correctly, but I don’t feel deeply attached to the item itself. It’s more that I like to be sure I’m not making a mistake.\n\nSo, in the way you’re asking it—significant distress about discarding, even useless things—I’d say no, I haven’t noticed that in myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating words, phrases, or numbers in your head, or feel the need to do something mentally a certain way to reduce anxiety, even if it's not connected to checking or organizing physical objects?\n\nPatient: Not really in a separate way, no. I mean, I can’t think of myself sitting there repeating a word or a number in my head to make something feel safe. The thing that keeps happening—the thing that really brought me in—is still tied to checking.\n\nIt’s those boxes, or the lists, or the drawers at the condo. I’ll look once, and I’ll know I looked. Then I’ll be ready to move on, or at least I’ll think I’m ready, and then this doubt comes back in: “Did I actually check that properly?” And before I can leave it alone, I end up checking again. Sometimes my wife is right there waiting for me, and I know she wants me to just let it be, and honestly I want to let it be too. But I don’t quite feel able to move on until I’ve gone back over it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s words or numbers in my head, exactly. It’s more that mental uncertainty that pushes me back into checking the same things, even when I know perfectly well I already did.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… yes, that’s exactly it, really. I’ll know there’s no sensible reason to be worried. I mean, with the boxes or the lists or those drawers at the condo, I can look at them and know, on one level, that I already checked. It’s not that I truly believe something terrible is sitting there waiting to happen every time. It’s more that I don’t feel able to move on until I’ve checked again, just to quiet that doubt.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F42.1, F42.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, what finally made me come in is this business of checking things, and I know that sounds small when I say it out loud, but it’s become... it’s become hard to just go about my day. I’ll check the same boxes, or lists I’ve made, or drawers in the condo, and then I’ll have to check them again before I can move on. And the frustrating part is, I know perfectly well I already looked. I can remember looking. But somehow that doesn’t settle it.\n\nMy wife will be standing there waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I want to, I really do, but I get caught up in making sure one more time. Then one more after that. It’s not that anything dramatic happens, exactly, but it slows everything down and makes me feel rather foolish and stuck. That’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Besides checking things, do you find yourself having other repetitive thoughts or feeling like you have to perform certain actions a specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I’m not sure if it’s exactly the same sort of thing. It’s more these thoughts that get stuck in my head, nagging at me. For example, I’ll start thinking I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files — some paper or note or record that I might need, even though realistically I know I probably don’t need it anymore. Or I’ll worry that I put a tool in the wrong box, and then later I won’t be able to find it when I need it.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can tell myself, “Now, come on, that’s unlikely,” or “You would remember if you’d thrown that out,” but it doesn’t quite land. The thought comes back again, and then I feel like I can’t really settle down or move on until I’ve somehow reassured myself about it. It’s not always loud, if that makes sense, but it’s persistent. It sort of keeps tapping me on the shoulder.\n\nDoctor: When you have these recurring thoughts, do they lead you to do certain things—like checking, organizing, or repeating actions—to try to feel better or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what happens, I suppose. The thought will come up, and then I feel I have to do something with it, not just let it sit there. So I’ll go back and check the same box, or the same list, or a drawer in the condo, even if I’ve already opened it and looked inside.\n\nAnd I know I’ve already looked — that’s the maddening part. It isn’t that I’ve forgotten in the usual sense. I can remember standing there and checking it. But there’s still this feeling of, “Well, maybe just make sure once more,” and then once more after that. It’s as if I can’t quite move on to the next thing until I’ve done it enough times, though I couldn’t tell you what “enough” is.\n\nMy wife will be waiting for me, sometimes literally standing there ready to go, and I can see that she wants me to just leave it alone. I want to leave it alone too. But I get pulled back into checking, and it’s embarrassing, really, because part of me is watching myself do it and thinking, “You already did this.” But I still feel I have to check before I can settle.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and checking behaviors cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life, such as taking up a lot of time or affecting your work or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does interfere, though I’d call it more wearing and time-consuming than catastrophic, if that distinction makes sense. The biggest example lately is unpacking the condo. That should have been a fairly ordinary job — boxes, shelves, sorting things into drawers — but it has stretched on much longer than it had any right to, because I keep stopping to verify what’s in a box, or where I put something, or whether I put it in the correct place.\n\nAnd then because I haven’t “finished” that, or because it doesn’t feel finished enough, I put off other things. I do some library tutoring preparation, or at least I’m supposed to, and I’ll find myself not starting it because I feel I have to settle the condo things first. Same with hobbies — things I used to just sit down and do, I’ll delay because there’s this sense that I can’t move on until I’m sure enough about whatever I was checking.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting my days. It’s not ruining everything, but it eats up time and makes ordinary tasks feel much heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you need to perform certain actions a specific number of times, in a certain way, or until it feels 'just right,' even if it's not directly related to what you're worried about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the “until it feels right” part fits more than a particular number. I don’t usually say to myself, “I have to do this three times,” or anything as clear as that. It’s more that I check the same boxes, or the same list, or the condo drawers again and again until I can somehow let myself move on.\n\nAnd that’s what really brought me in, honestly. I’ll know perfectly well that I already looked. I can remember opening the drawer, seeing what was inside, closing it. But then I’ll be halfway to the door, or my wife will be standing there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I get this pull to go back and make sure one more time. Not because there’s some new reason, exactly. Just because it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know it looks unnecessary, and I know it is unnecessary in a practical sense. But in the moment, leaving it unchecked feels harder than going back and doing it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to arrange things in a certain way, make sure things are perfectly aligned, or repeat actions until they feel exactly right, even if it’s not about checking for safety or preventing something bad?\n\nPatient: Not so much the arranging or lining things up, no. I don’t really have a thing where objects have to be perfectly straight or symmetrical, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nFor me it’s much more the repeating until I can move on. What brought me in is that I keep finding myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same condo drawers over and over. I know perfectly well I already looked — I can remember looking — but it still doesn’t feel settled. My wife might be standing there waiting for me, and I know she wants me to just leave it be, and I want that too. But I’ll still feel pulled back to check it again, almost like I need that last look before I can go on to the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don’t want or welcome—thoughts that feel intrusive or upsetting, even if you’re not always acting on them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. They’re not dramatic thoughts, exactly, but they’re intrusive in the sense that I don’t invite them and I can’t seem to just dismiss them when they show up.\n\nA common one is this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files — some paper or note or folder that I’ll need later, even if I haven’t needed it in years. Or I’ll think maybe I put a tool in the wrong box while unpacking, and then the thought just sits there and bothers me. I’ll tell myself, “Well, that’s probably not true,” or “If it were really important, I’d know,” but it doesn’t quite take. It comes back around again, and then I have trouble settling down and moving on to whatever I was trying to do next.\n\nIt’s upsetting mostly because it feels unreasonable, and I know that. I can argue with it logically, but it still keeps tapping me on the shoulder, so to speak.\n\nDoctor: When you have these intrusive thoughts, do you feel driven to do something — like checking, repeating, or reassuring yourself — to try to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly the pattern, I think. The thought comes in, and then I feel like I have to do something to quiet it down — usually checking.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, and the drawers at the condo over and over before I can move on. And the odd thing is, I do know I already looked. I’m not confused about it. I can remember looking in the drawer, or going through the list, or checking the box, and still it feels like there’s this unfinished feeling, like I can’t quite trust it unless I look again.\n\nIt’s not that my wife hasn’t been patient, but she’ll be standing there waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I can see how unnecessary it looks from the outside. I know perfectly well that I already checked. But in the moment, checking again feels like the only way to get that anxious feeling to settle enough so I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to throw things away or feel a need to keep items longer than you think you reasonably should, even if they don’t seem useful to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as someone who keeps things just for the sake of keeping them. I mean, I have old papers and household things like anyone my age would, and sometimes I’ll hesitate before throwing something out, especially if it seems like it might have some future use. But I wouldn’t say I have piles of useless things that I can’t part with, or that rooms are taken over by belongings, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWith teaching materials, for example, there are things I’ve kept longer than someone else might think is necessary, but in my mind they had some possible value or history attached to them. So I don’t know if that counts. It doesn’t feel like collecting or hoarding to me. It’s more that I can get stuck wondering whether something might turn out to matter later, and then I’m slower to toss it. But as a general need to keep everything, no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to get rid of items, even ones that seem useless or have no real value, because the idea of throwing them away feels unsettling or causes you distress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that way. I mean, I can hesitate with things, especially if I get into that frame of mind of, “Well, what if I need this later?” But if something is truly useless to me, or has no real value that I can see, I don’t usually feel distressed in the sense of being unable to let it go.\n\nSometimes I may take longer than I should deciding, because I’ll sort of review what it is, where it came from, whether it belongs with other papers or household items, that sort of thing. My wife would probably say I can make too much of a small decision. But it’s not that the object itself feels impossible to part with. It’s more the uncertainty around whether I’m making the right decision.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I have a strong distress about throwing away useless things just because they’re being thrown away. It’s more that I can get bogged down in deciding whether they really are useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you have to perform certain mental acts—like counting, praying, or going over something in your mind—a certain way or a certain number of times to prevent something bad from happening or to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not so much counting or praying, no. It’s more… going back over it in my head only seems to happen around the checking. Like with the boxes or the lists or the drawers at the condo, I’ll know I already looked, and I can even picture myself having looked. But then my mind sort of replays it and says, “Are you sure? Did you really see it properly?” And then I feel pushed to check again.\n\nIt isn’t usually a set number, like three times or seven times. It’s more that I don’t feel done until it feels settled, if that makes sense. My wife will be waiting for me, and I’ll know she wants me to just leave it be, and part of me agrees with her completely. But the anxious feeling doesn’t really let go until I’ve checked again, even though I know perfectly well I already did.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to discard items, even ones that seem to have no real value, because you feel a strong need to save them or because it causes you significant distress to throw them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s really a big issue for me, no. I mean, I may pause over things longer than is sensible, especially papers or old household items where I’m thinking, “Well, maybe this could be useful,” or “Maybe I should check what this is before tossing it.” But it’s not usually because I feel some powerful need to save it, or because throwing it away feels intolerable.\n\nIf something really has no value and I can identify it as trash, I can get rid of it. I might take a little too long deciding, and sometimes I’ll second-guess whether I’ve sorted it correctly, but I don’t feel deeply attached to the item itself. It’s more that I like to be sure I’m not making a mistake.\n\nSo, in the way you’re asking it—significant distress about discarding, even useless things—I’d say no, I haven’t noticed that in myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating words, phrases, or numbers in your head, or feel the need to do something mentally a certain way to reduce anxiety, even if it's not connected to checking or organizing physical objects?\n\nPatient: Not really in a separate way, no. I mean, I can’t think of myself sitting there repeating a word or a number in my head to make something feel safe. The thing that keeps happening—the thing that really brought me in—is still tied to checking.\n\nIt’s those boxes, or the lists, or the drawers at the condo. I’ll look once, and I’ll know I looked. Then I’ll be ready to move on, or at least I’ll think I’m ready, and then this doubt comes back in: “Did I actually check that properly?” And before I can leave it alone, I end up checking again. Sometimes my wife is right there waiting for me, and I know she wants me to just let it be, and honestly I want to let it be too. But I don’t quite feel able to move on until I’ve gone back over it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s words or numbers in my head, exactly. It’s more that mental uncertainty that pushes me back into checking the same things, even when I know perfectly well I already did.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you have to check things even when there’s no real reason to worry about them, just to make sure your mind feels at ease?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s exactly it, really. I’ll know there’s no sensible reason to be worried. I mean, with the boxes or the lists or those drawers at the condo, I can look at them and know, on one level, that I already checked. Sometimes I can even remember the moment of checking, like I can picture my hand opening the drawer or my eyes going down the list.\n\nBut then there’s this little unsettled feeling that comes right back, almost like my mind won’t accept it as finished. It’s not that I truly believe something terrible is sitting there waiting to happen every time. It’s more that I don’t feel able to move on until I’ve checked again, just to quiet that doubt.\n\nAnd my wife will be there waiting for me, and I can tell she wants me to leave it alone, and honestly I want to leave it alone too. I’m not enjoying it. I know it looks unnecessary. But I end up going back over the same things anyway, because otherwise my mind keeps nagging at me that maybe I missed something, even though I know perfectly well I already looked.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to discard items, even ones that seem to have no real value, because you feel a strong need to save them or because it causes you significant distress to throw them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s really a big issue for me, no. I mean, I can’t think of myself sitting there repeating a word or a number in my head to make something feel safe. The thing that keeps happening—the thing that really brought me in—is still tied to checking.\n\nIt’s those boxes, or the lists, or the drawers at the condo. I’ll look once, and I’ll know I looked. Sometimes my wife is right there waiting for me, and I know she wants me to just let it be, and honestly I want to let it be too.   {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I’d say it feels much more like something I’m resisting, or at least trying to resist, than something I think is a good idea. I don’t sit there thinking, “Oh, this is useful, I should do this again.” It’s more like I’ve already checked the box, or the list, or the drawer at the condo, and I know I checked it — I really do know it — but then I get this uncomfortable doubt that sort of keeps tugging at me.

And I try to move on. Especially if my wife is there waiting, which she often is, and I can feel that she wants me to just leave it alone and go. I want to do that too. But I’ll be standing there thinking, “Just walk away, you already looked,” and then almost immediately another part of me says, “But what if you didn’t look carefully enough?” or “What if you missed something?” So I end up going back and checking again.

It’s not enjoyable. Not at all. It’s frustrating, honestly, because it makes me feel a bit foolish. I’m not getting pleasure from opening the same drawer again or going over the same list again. It’s just that checking seems to quiet that anxious, unsettled feeling for a little while, enough that I can move on — though sometimes only after doing it more than once.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF42.2, F42.1, F42.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, what finally made me come in is this business of checking things, and I know that sounds small when I say it out loud, but it’s become... it’s become hard to just go about my day. I’ll check the same boxes, or lists I’ve made, or drawers in the condo, and then I’ll have to check them again before I can move on. And the frustrating part is, I know perfectly well I already looked. I can remember looking. But somehow that doesn’t settle it.\n\nMy wife will be standing there waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I want to, I really do, but I get caught up in making sure one more time. Then one more after that. It’s not that anything dramatic happens, exactly, but it slows everything down and makes me feel rather foolish and stuck. That’s really why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Besides checking things, do you find yourself having other repetitive thoughts or feeling like you have to perform certain actions a specific way?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I’m not sure if it’s exactly the same sort of thing. It’s more these thoughts that get stuck in my head, nagging at me. For example, I’ll start thinking I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files — some paper or note or record that I might need, even though realistically I know I probably don’t need it anymore. Or I’ll worry that I put a tool in the wrong box, and then later I won’t be able to find it when I need it.\n\nAnd the thing is, I can tell myself, “Now, come on, that’s unlikely,” or “You would remember if you’d thrown that out,” but it doesn’t quite land. The thought comes back again, and then I feel like I can’t really settle down or move on until I’ve somehow reassured myself about it. It’s not always loud, if that makes sense, but it’s persistent. It sort of keeps tapping me on the shoulder.\n\nDoctor: When you have these recurring thoughts, do they lead you to do certain things—like checking, organizing, or repeating actions—to try to feel better or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly what happens, I suppose. The thought will come up, and then I feel I have to do something with it, not just let it sit there. So I’ll go back and check the same box, or the same list, or a drawer in the condo, even if I’ve already opened it and looked inside.\n\nAnd I know I’ve already looked — that’s the maddening part. It isn’t that I’ve forgotten in the usual sense. I can remember standing there and checking it. But there’s still this feeling of, “Well, maybe just make sure once more,” and then once more after that. It’s as if I can’t quite move on to the next thing until I’ve done it enough times, though I couldn’t tell you what “enough” is.\n\nMy wife will be waiting for me, sometimes literally standing there ready to go, and I can see that she wants me to just leave it alone. I want to leave it alone too. But I get pulled back into checking, and it’s embarrassing, really, because part of me is watching myself do it and thinking, “You already did this.” But I still feel I have to check before I can settle.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and checking behaviors cause you significant distress or interfere with your daily life, such as taking up a lot of time or affecting your work or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it does interfere, though I’d call it more wearing and time-consuming than catastrophic, if that distinction makes sense. The biggest example lately is unpacking the condo. That should have been a fairly ordinary job — boxes, shelves, sorting things into drawers — but it has stretched on much longer than it had any right to, because I keep stopping to verify what’s in a box, or where I put something, or whether I put it in the correct place.\n\nAnd then because I haven’t “finished” that, or because it doesn’t feel finished enough, I put off other things. I do some library tutoring preparation, or at least I’m supposed to, and I’ll find myself not starting it because I feel I have to settle the condo things first. Same with hobbies — things I used to just sit down and do, I’ll delay because there’s this sense that I can’t move on until I’m sure enough about whatever I was checking.\n\nSo yes, it’s affecting my days. It’s not ruining everything, but it eats up time and makes ordinary tasks feel much heavier than they should.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you need to perform certain actions a specific number of times, in a certain way, or until it feels 'just right,' even if it's not directly related to what you're worried about?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think the “until it feels right” part fits more than a particular number. I don’t usually say to myself, “I have to do this three times,” or anything as clear as that. It’s more that I check the same boxes, or the same list, or the condo drawers again and again until I can somehow let myself move on.\n\nAnd that’s what really brought me in, honestly. I’ll know perfectly well that I already looked. I can remember opening the drawer, seeing what was inside, closing it. But then I’ll be halfway to the door, or my wife will be standing there waiting for me to just leave it be, and I get this pull to go back and make sure one more time. Not because there’s some new reason, exactly. Just because it doesn’t feel settled.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because I know it looks unnecessary, and I know it is unnecessary in a practical sense. But in the moment, leaving it unchecked feels harder than going back and doing it again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel the need to arrange things in a certain way, make sure things are perfectly aligned, or repeat actions until they feel exactly right, even if it’s not about checking for safety or preventing something bad?\n\nPatient: Not so much the arranging or lining things up, no. I don’t really have a thing where objects have to be perfectly straight or symmetrical, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nFor me it’s much more the repeating until I can move on. What brought me in is that I keep finding myself checking the same boxes, the same lists, the same condo drawers over and over. I know perfectly well I already looked — I can remember looking — but it still doesn’t feel settled. My wife might be standing there waiting for me, and I know she wants me to just leave it be, and I want that too. But I’ll still feel pulled back to check it again, almost like I need that last look before I can go on to the next thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have thoughts that come into your mind that you don’t want or welcome—thoughts that feel intrusive or upsetting, even if you’re not always acting on them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. They’re not dramatic thoughts, exactly, but they’re intrusive in the sense that I don’t invite them and I can’t seem to just dismiss them when they show up.\n\nA common one is this nagging thought that I may have thrown away something important from my old teaching files — some paper or note or folder that I’ll need later, even if I haven’t needed it in years. Or I’ll think maybe I put a tool in the wrong box while unpacking, and then the thought just sits there and bothers me. I’ll tell myself, “Well, that’s probably not true,” or “If it were really important, I’d know,” but it doesn’t quite take. It comes back around again, and then I have trouble settling down and moving on to whatever I was trying to do next.\n\nIt’s upsetting mostly because it feels unreasonable, and I know that. I can argue with it logically, but it still keeps tapping me on the shoulder, so to speak.\n\nDoctor: When you have these intrusive thoughts, do you feel driven to do something — like checking, repeating, or reassuring yourself — to try to reduce the anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s exactly the pattern, I think. The thought comes in, and then I feel like I have to do something to quiet it down — usually checking.\n\nWhat brought me in is that I keep checking the same boxes, the same lists, and the drawers at the condo over and over before I can move on. And the odd thing is, I do know I already looked. I’m not confused about it. I can remember looking in the drawer, or going through the list, or checking the box, and still it feels like there’s this unfinished feeling, like I can’t quite trust it unless I look again.\n\nIt’s not that my wife hasn’t been patient, but she’ll be standing there waiting for me to just leave it alone, and I can see how unnecessary it looks from the outside. I know perfectly well that I already checked. But in the moment, checking again feels like the only way to get that anxious feeling to settle enough so I can move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to throw things away or feel a need to keep items longer than you think you reasonably should, even if they don’t seem useful to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not entirely sure how to answer that, because I don’t think of myself as someone who keeps things just for the sake of keeping them. I mean, I have old papers and household things like anyone my age would, and sometimes I’ll hesitate before throwing something out, especially if it seems like it might have some future use. But I wouldn’t say I have piles of useless things that I can’t part with, or that rooms are taken over by belongings, if that’s what you mean.\n\nWith teaching materials, for example, there are things I’ve kept longer than someone else might think is necessary, but in my mind they had some possible value or history attached to them. So I don’t know if that counts. It doesn’t feel like collecting or hoarding to me. It’s more that I can get stuck wondering whether something might turn out to matter later, and then I’m slower to toss it. But as a general need to keep everything, no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to get rid of items, even ones that seem useless or have no real value, because the idea of throwing them away feels unsettling or causes you distress?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in that way. I mean, I can hesitate with things, especially if I get into that frame of mind of, “Well, what if I need this later?” But if something is truly useless to me, or has no real value that I can see, I don’t usually feel distressed in the sense of being unable to let it go.\n\nSometimes I may take longer than I should deciding, because I’ll sort of review what it is, where it came from, whether it belongs with other papers or household items, that sort of thing. My wife would probably say I can make too much of a small decision. But it’s not that the object itself feels impossible to part with. It’s more the uncertainty around whether I’m making the right decision.\n\nSo, no, I wouldn’t say I have a strong distress about throwing away useless things just because they’re being thrown away. It’s more that I can get bogged down in deciding whether they really are useless.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel that you have to perform certain mental acts—like counting, praying, or going over something in your mind—a certain way or a certain number of times to prevent something bad from happening or to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: Not so much counting or praying, no. It’s more… going back over it in my head only seems to happen around the checking. Like with the boxes or the lists or the drawers at the condo, I’ll know I already looked, and I can even picture myself having looked. But then my mind sort of replays it and says, “Are you sure? Did you really see it properly?” And then I feel pushed to check again.\n\nIt isn’t usually a set number, like three times or seven times. It’s more that I don’t feel done until it feels settled, if that makes sense. My wife will be waiting for me, and I’ll know she wants me to just leave it be, and part of me agrees with her completely. But the anxious feeling doesn’t really let go until I’ve checked again, even though I know perfectly well I already did.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it difficult to discard items, even ones that seem to have no real value, because you feel a strong need to save them or because it causes you significant distress to throw them away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s really a big issue for me, no. I mean, I may pause over things longer than is sensible, especially papers or old household items where I’m thinking, “Well, maybe this could be useful,” or “Maybe I should check what this is before tossing it.” But it’s not usually because I feel some powerful need to save it, or because throwing it away feels intolerable.\n\nIf something really has no value and I can identify it as trash, I can get rid of it. I might take a little too long deciding, and sometimes I’ll second-guess whether I’ve sorted it correctly, but I don’t feel deeply attached to the item itself. It’s more that I like to be sure I’m not making a mistake.\n\nSo, in the way you’re asking it—significant distress about discarding, even useless things—I’d say no, I haven’t noticed that in myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating words, phrases, or numbers in your head, or feel the need to do something mentally a certain way to reduce anxiety, even if it's not connected to checking or organizing physical objects?\n\nPatient: Not really in a separate way, no. I mean, I can’t think of myself sitting there repeating a word or a number in my head to make something feel safe. The thing that keeps happening—the thing that really brought me in—is still tied to checking.\n\nIt’s those boxes, or the lists, or the drawers at the condo. I’ll look once, and I’ll know I looked. Then I’ll be ready to move on, or at least I’ll think I’m ready, and then this doubt comes back in: “Did I actually check that properly?” And before I can leave it alone, I end up checking again. Sometimes my wife is right there waiting for me, and I know she wants me to just let it be, and honestly I want to let it be too. But I don’t quite feel able to move on until I’ve gone back over it.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s words or numbers in my head, exactly. That should have been a fairly ordinary job — boxes, shelves, sorting things into drawers — but it has stretched on much longer than it had any right to, because I keep stopping to verify what’s in a box, or where I put something, or whether I put it in the correct place.\n\nAnd then because I haven’t “finished” that, or because it doesn’t feel finished enough, I put off other things.
